“Why?” 

“You don’t want to find out.” 

They had nearly reached the kitchen when Agatha heard her name called. She couldn’t help but detach her grip on Rio’s hand as she turned around to find Lilia approaching them both with a suspicion on her face that told Agatha she had seen enough. 

“Agatha, darling. You’ve been avoiding me.” 

“Oh, so she doesn’t just do it with me?” Rio chuckled the words under her breath. 

Lilia turned to Rio, taking the woman in before a wry smile appeared at her cheeks. “And who is this striking young lady, Agatha? You have no manners, child.” 

Agatha glared at Lilia, but the woman did not budge. She could already see the satisfaction on Lilia’s face, a smugness that meant she was not getting away from this situation lightly. 

None of them were. 

“Lilia,” Agatha began through gritted teeth. “This is Rio. An old friend.” 

She pretended not to notice the raised brow both women offered her in return. 

“I know all of your friends. Why has she kept me from you, mia cara?”

“Jamás lograré entender a Agatha Harkness,” Rio spoke in a way that had Agatha wishing she hadn’t dropped out of Spanish classes; she was taken about, and even more absurdly so: mesmerised. “I will never understand the ways of Agatha Harkness.” 

Lilia stared at Rio, looking her up and down before her lips curled into a smile. Agatha watched on, realising she would not hear the end of this for a long time. 

“Me caes bien. Dime, ¿cuándo conociste a nuestra queridísima Agatha?” Lilia said. “I like you. Tell me, when did you meet our dearest Agatha?” 

Rio laughed. “Hace doce años, esta misma noche de hecho,” she said, glancing towards Agatha who was only becoming more frustrated by the second at the two women conversing in a language at no means accessible to herself. “Twelve years ago, this evening actually.” 

Agatha coughed, the point of her heel tapping against the floor. “Hola? I am still here, you know.” 

Lilia chose to ignore her, instead turning back towards Rio. “¿Qué haces mañana? ¿Supongo que estarás con tu familia?” she asked. “Rio, what are you doing tomorrow? I expect you will be with family?” 

Agatha noticed when Rio’s eyes dropped to the floor briefly, and the way one of her fingers began to trace the rim of her glass without stopping. She watched her take a deep breath, hold it for a few seconds, before shaking the hair from her eyes and exhaling. 

There was a story there, for sure. Even if she couldn’t understand what was actually being said.

“No. Estaré sola, por desgracia. Mis padres ya no están entre nosotros.” Rio looked back up, calm and composed as ever. “No. It is just me, unfortunately. My parents are no longer with us.” 

“Oh, querida. Por favor, ven con Agatha. Comeremos y beberemos y olvidaremos el pasado juntas. Además, quiero que me cuentes cómo conoces a mi hija.” Lilia said. “Oh, my dear. Please, come over with Agatha. We will eat and drink and forget about the past together. Plus, I want to hear about how you know my daughter.” 

“¿Hija?” Rio dared to ask. Agatha had always spoken of a mother that she despised; this woman seemed quite the opposite. “Daughter?” 

“Ah, como si lo fuera. Una historia para otro momento,” Lilia waved her hand away from herself, smiling as she looked at Agatha. “Ah, as good as. A story for another time.”

Agatha’s hand found Rio’s elbow, fingers pinching at her skin beneath her blazer to get her attention. “What has she just asked you to do?” 

Rio laughed again. “Spend Christmas Day with you.” 

Agatha nearly spat out the mouthful of sparkling water she had drank in the hopes she would appear unbothered by their little bilingual exchange. She took Lilia’s wrist into her hand, before offering Rio a flat smile. “Excuse us for one moment.” 

She dragged Lilia a few steps away. “What are you doing?” 

“Extending a kind invite.” 

“Yes. To a stranger.” 

“Darling, she is no stranger to you. Don’t lie to me.” 

She knew it was wrong to argue; she knew Lilia saw everything the average person could not. It was her biggest frustration with the woman: she could not hide a damn thing from her. 

“It’s not me I am talking about.” 

“I saw the way you were looking at her earlier.” 

“And I have already told you to get your eyesight checked.” 

“I doubt opticians have much of an understanding for the third eye.” 

Agatha rolled her eyes before holding her palm against her head. “Fine,” she hissed. “But I am telling you once - please - no pictures of him, no mention of him, nothing at all.” 

Lilia remained silent. 

“Please, Lilia,” she whispered, softer now. “Not yet. It is too soon.” 

“Alright, alright,” she replied with a sincerity in her eye that allowed Agatha to breathe a little easier. “How long has this been going on?”  

“Has what been going on?” 

“You and Rio.”

“There is no me and Rio.” 

“Oh, Agatha. You truly are an awful liar.”

“Hard to be an awful liar when you are not lying.” 

“You are. Your nose crinkles in just the same way his used to when he had taken one more piece of candy than he was supposed to.”  

Agatha exhaled, unable to help the small smile that appeared at the thought of her little boy. She didn’t mind being caught out if it was because of him. 

“Now take me back to that darling girl of yours. She is rather lovely. Very you.” 

“Oh my god,” Agatha muttered under her breath as she watched Lilia already returning to where they had abandoned Rio. She caught Rio’s eye as they approached, and glared back at her in a warning to not push her luck. When Rio showed off her dimpled smile and a slight shake of her head, and there was a slight fluttering within her chest, Agatha began to wonder what on earth she was getting herself into. 

Mara thankfully came to steal Lilia away from them, and Agatha found herself being led through the condo with Rio on her elbow, in search of Alice. 

Agatha had never thought much of Alice; they just weren’t two people who would usually mix with one another if it weren’t for their incestuous post-college friendship group. She knew Alice had never liked her, which was a feeling she was used to. She had treated Rio like shit in the past, and present, there was no denying. Besides, Agatha was sure that Rio had caught her best friend up on recent reunions. 

Then again, Agatha didn’t like her much either because she had been the one to pick up the pieces of Jen’s heart during their first, and second breakup, which had both been rather inconvenient for her. 

“Agatha,” Alice nodded her head slightly too curtly. 

Agatha offered her an equally curt smile. “Alice, pleased to see you managed to worm your way in again.” 

She ignored the jab into her hip from Rio’s elbow. 

“Could say the same about you.” 

Agatha turned to Rio, brows raised. “I do believe it was you that asked me for that drink?” 

Rio just held her hands up defensively. 

“Well it's nice to see you,” Agatha began. “Unless you decide to walk out on Jen again.’’

“Same for you with Rio.”

Agatha thanked a god she didn’t believe in when a chiming of metal against glass sounded throughout the condo; Jen had climbed onto a chair, martini in hand, waiting for all eyes to be on her. 

“Everybody, thank you. You all look stunning. Thank you so much for being here - I’ll try to keep this short, but you all know I am a woman of many words”

That’s for damn sure, Agatha thought. 

“I first started gathering friends and family for Christmas Eve because - well - because no one else bothered too.” 

Laughter echoed across the walls. 

 “Some of you have been here from the start when we were slightly more reckless with our fun,” she looked over at Agatha, stood with Alice and Rio, and nodded her glass to them, “and some of you have joined us in more recent years as we continue the weird journey that is life.” 

Agatha felt a hand grasp her elbow gently, the touch barely there. She looked at Rio as the both withheld a laugh at Jen’s words. 

“I wish everyone beautiful festivities, and even more beautiful lives as we head into a new year.”

Rio nudged her side when she caught Agatha rolling her eyes. 

“Before I finish, I want to take a moment to remember those unable to be with us this evening. To absent friends, and even more absent little loves.” 

Oh. 

She was going to lose it, right there in front of a room full of strangers and the one person she hoped would never see the facade of Agatha Harkness break down before her. 

She didn’t look at Rio that time. She couldn’t repeat the toast in chorus with the other guests. She could barely muster to gulp down the water in her mouth. Instead, she briefly closed her eyes and imagined her boy, clinging to her arms as his head lolled off her shoulder in sleep. 

My little love, she thought to herself. 

When Rio’s hand found the small of her back for no longer than a few seconds, she asked no questions and Agatha offered no reaction to the gesture, even though a warmth spread through her. 

The night soon began to wrap up, guests dribbling out after exhuberent goodbyes to Jen. 

Agatha and Rio found themselves gorging on the remainders of Jen’s spread; the spring rolls were moorish and Agatha was famished. She was also exhausted, and her social battery was in desperate need of recovery. Not to mention, she wanted to go home and see her boy. It was Christmas after all. 

“About tomorrow, you don’t have to come. To Lilia’s, I mean,” Agatha said. 

Rio looked up, before speaking mid-mouthful. “I mean, I have nothing better to do.” 

They both laughed at her nonsenical words. 

“I think you’ll realise that nothing is a lot better than being at Lilia’s.” 

Agatha tried to ignore the way Rio’s eyes sank slightly. 

“You don’t want me there?” 

She shrugged her shoulders. “Christmas Day feels a bit soon, does it not?” 

Rio laughed. “It’s just a day. Thought you hated it.” 

“I do.” 

“Cool. Then we can hate it together,” Rio said. She held up another spring roll, offering it to Agatha. “Ok?

Agatha hesitated, before sighing and accepting the roll. “Fine.”  

She couldn’t hide her smile. 

“You could come over tonight,” Rio mentioned with nonchalance. “If you don’t walk out on me again, that is.” 

Agatha stilled. For some reason, her usual, quick-witted way had clearly decided to take a vacation because she had no response. She did want to stay with Rio, for as long as possible, even she couldn’t deny that. But she also wanted to see her boy, and she couldn’t exactly explain that one without inviting one hundred more questions and ruining the small bridge they had just begun to build. 

With all the divine timing in the world, Agatha felt a hand at her shoulder. She turned to see Lilia had approached them both, her gaze watching them intently and in a way Agatha had never seen on the woman before. 

“I was just coming to say my goodbyes, am I interrupting?” 

Agatha immediately shook her head, whilst Rio shrugged her shoulders. 

“I was just asking Agatha what she was doing this evening-”

“Oh she has nothing of importance to do.” 

“Lilia,” Agatha warned. “You don’t know that.” 

Lilia’s stare hardened. “What is more important than keeping Rio company? You can’t expect her to make her own way over tomorrow.” 

“She’s a big girl, I’m sure she can figure it out-”

“Agatha,” Lilia’s voice left no room for argument. “I’m sure some company would do you good.” 

Agatha glared at Lilia, who only offered her a brief smirk. 

Oh this woman knew exactly what she was doing, and especially what she was keeping Agatha from doing in turn. 

“I’ll see you both tomorrow,” Lilia said as she brushed a stray hair from Agatha’s temple and in turn was swatted away. 

“So, yours then?” 

Rio’s hands found the pockets of her blazer, eyes finding the floor. “You don’t have to.” 

“No, I don’t. Which makes it even more absurd that I am considering it. Come on,” Agatha held out her hand, helping Rio from the counter they had both perched on and slipped from the evening without bidding farewell to their host.

Chapter 6

Chapter Summary

Agatha and Rio spend Christmas Day together.

Chapter Notes

i want to apologise for the delay; i had too much fun in the u.s over the last two weeks and ended up not writing at all

also i spent an hour editing this and then didn't save it? so spent another hour editing it and my eyes hurt and it is not as good as the first edit. please cry with me!

oh and i went to nyc. i've been hesitant to set this story in a physical place but ive experienced the city now so fuck it they are in nyc and please forgive me for their excessive use of cabs and being able to afford their own apartments and the absolute lack of knowledge of proxemties and neighbourhoods and realism lmao

See the end of the chapter for more notes

It was only after leaving the party that Agatha decided she would go back to Rio’s place. 

Her original plan after Lilia’s unneeded matchmaking was to excuse herself at the last minute and order her own cab.

That was until they stepped out on the snow-filled sidewalk, not another soul on the block, and realised how cold the wind was and that they had no means of getting back into Jen’s building. 

Rio noticed the chill across Agatha’s skin. A mesh shirt would do that to you, and for reasons Agatha could not explain, she had not brought a coat with her.

Without a word, Rio took off her own blazer and wrapped it over Agatha’s shoulders, despite her immediate protests. 

That was the first move to weaken Agatha’s resolve. 

When Rio directed her to the building wall in a spot hidden from the wind, she placed the palm of her hand against Agatha’s chest and pushed her further into the marble. 

“Rio, what are you-” 

“Shush,” Rio whispered. 

And then she kissed her. 

It was slow but with an intensity that they both had been avoiding.

It wasn’t in the heat of the moment, nor like the sloppy messes of their one-night stands.

It wasn’t as hesitant as when Rio kissed her cheek those few weeks ago, or when Agatha finally kissed her that evening but cut it short through fear of who would see. 

It was, like the movies say, as if the world melted away around them. 

Agatha wasn’t cold anymore. She couldn’t register the fear that usually controlled her mind. In that moment, it was only her lips against Rio’s and their hands reaching for each other. 

She tried to ignore the absence she felt the moment they parted. 

All she knew was that she wanted to do it again, and that meant following Rio home. 

“What was that for?” 

“You look very kissable right now,” Rio said as she gripped the lapels of her blazer over Agatha’s chest. Her grin was wide and her eyes glinted in a way that made her far too endearing. Agatha found it hard to look anywhere else. “Had to do better than your performance earlier.” 

Agatha rolled her eyes. “You wanted me to shove my tongue down your throat in front of all those people?”

Rio looked at her sheepishly, her lips curving into a small smirk. “I don’t think I would complain about it if you did.” 

“Rio, it's freezing. Just order the damn car.” 

“Yes Ma’am.” 

 

 

She couldn’t deny it, spending the night with Rio had not been as bad as she feared. 

In fact, it had not been bad at all. It hadn’t even been good. It had been close to perfect. 

They returned to Rio’s apartment and Agatha quickly found herself being pulled into Rio’s bedroom. She was guided to sit on the bed and watched as Rio hurled items of clothing from her closet until she held out a pair of sweatpants and an old Columbia sweater. 

“Before you make one of your snide comments, no I’m not trying to get you naked,” she said as she placed the clothes beside Agatha and moved back to her closet. “Change, before you become hypothermic. Bathrooms through there.” 

Agatha raised her eyebrows. 

Rio’s assertion made her feel things she could not deny, but what hit her even more was the sincere realisation that Rio had offered her space and privacy.

Despite her abrupt exit two weeks before, Rio had accepted her apology, Rio had tried to work through it, and had clearly taken heed of what Agatha needed most. 

It left her unsettled. 

It left her considering how they could ever get another chance with one another with the secret she was carrying still buried beneath her shirt. 

Those thoughts began to make her decision over the matter a little easier. 

“Agatha?” 

She was snapped from her thoughts, shaking her shoulders back and grabbing the clothes. 

“Thank you,” she narrowed her eyes as she stood up, “Doctor.” 

They spent time on the balcony together, Agatha wrapped up in three blankets and a cup of fruit tea in hand whilst Rio smoked a joint over the railings. Agatha practically salivated at the sight - of the joint, or of Rio, she would elaborate no further. 

Their conversation remained mundane. Rio asked her about a few guests at the party and Agatha tried to convince her again that she did not need to attend Lilia’s with her tomorrow. Rio remained adamant she had nothing better to do, much to Agatha’s dismay. When silence fell between them, it didn't leave Agatha itching to get out of her own skin. 

“What happened to them?” 

Agatha hadn’t quite realised the words left her own mouth until it was too late. 

Rio stilled and turned to look at her. “What?” 

“Your parents.” 

She knew she shouldn't have brought the matter up, but curiosity and one more look at that overly smiley young girl in the photo on Rio’s shelving had gotten the better of her.

And then there was that small hope within her that maybe she wasn’t the only one carrying baggage between them. 

Agatha watched Rio’s face fall before taking a long drag on her joint as she stared down onto the street below her. 

“Dead.” 

Agatha frowned.

From where she sat, she was able to outstretch her foot and tap it against Rio’s leg to get her attention.

Rio offered her a smile that didn’t quite reach her eyes when she saw Agatha hold out her hand, beckoning her to sit down beside her. 

“I had gathered that much,” Agatha spoke, lifting the blankets to cover Rio. “I shouldn’t have brought it-”

“No, it’s ok. I just don’t talk about them much. Or ever, for that fact. Alice knows and that’s it.” 

“You don’t have anyone else?” 

“Not here.”

“Here?” 

“They’re all back in Puerto-Rico. Well, what’s left of them.” 

Agatha made a mental note to ask about that admission another time. 

“They’re mad. Lovely, but mad. Way too much witchcraft under one roof; my Mom left at eighteen then met my Dad over here. Said she wanted a normal life for her future kids,” Rio spat out a laugh. “Guess she failed on that one.” 

“You think you’ve failed at a normal life? You can’t be serious.” 

“I’m single, child-less and married to my career at the age of thirty-six. Mami would be rolling in her grave if she hadn’t been scattered across the Atlantic. Not too proud of myself, either.” 

She shouldn’t say it. 

She should absolutely not say it. 

“... I didn’t take you for the kind of person who’d want kids.”

Rio shrugged her shoulders, completely oblivious to the intake of breath Agatha now held. 

“At some point I did,” she said. “Might get a cat. A friend for Victoria.” 

“I can’t believe you named that thing,” Agatha said, not before groaning at the reminder. 

She chose to avoid continuing their conversation in the direction it was going, quickly deciding she did not want or need to hear anymore.

It didn’t matter anyway. 

“I think they’d be proud of you,” Agatha offered after a period of silence. 

“You do?” 

Agatha nodded. “Minus the tarantula, that is.”

“They met her. They got her for me when I was seventeen,” Rio began. “Then they died six months after.” 

“That thing has been alive for nearly twenty years?” 

The disgusted look on Agatha’s face was enough to provoke a second laugh from Rio, despite the territory of the conversation. 

Rio nodded. “She won’t have much longer left in her, maybe a few years if I’m lucky. Life’s a cycle, or whatever bullshit it is that they try to tell us.” 

Agatha understood that all too well. 

“Who looked after you? When they were gone, I mean. You didn’t get put into…” 

Rio shook her head quickly. 

“My abuelita flew over so I had someone, even if it was only a couple of months until I turned eighteen. She passed away just before I got my acceptance letter to college. God bless inheritance and dead-parent scholarships, otherwise I would never have gotten into Columbia.” 

Agatha considered this news.

She imagined Rio, only a few years before she had met her for the first time, dealing with all that loss at such a young, formative age.

She imagined the smiley girl in the photo, and finally understood why she had never seen the woman smile that same way again. 

It made Agatha look at Rio from a different perspective, that was for sure. 

“I met Alice on the first day of college and eventually found out her Mom had died not too long before, so we co-founded the unofficial Dead Mom Club and the rest was history.” 

“You never mentioned them,” Agatha noted. 

“You never asked,” Rio stumped out the end of her joint before slapping her hands against her knees. “Come on, you need your beauty sleep.” 

Agatha wanted to protest and wanted to offer back a snarky remark; instead she remained quiet and took the hand offered to her before being lifted from her seat. 

Before Rio could move to the balcony doors, Agatha’s grip on her palm tightened as she pulled Rio back to face her. 

“Not so fast,” Agatha said softly. 

Rio looked up at her, their faces inches from one another now. Agatha noticed the hesitation in the usually-confident woman. The flicker of doubt behind those eyes, the slight knit of her brows. 

Her fingertips traced Rio’s jaw before she moved to brush back a loose lock of hair. 

The small whimper that met Agatha’s ears when she leant in and started to kiss Rio was a sound she wanted to hear over and over again.

She felt Rio relax into her; felt a hand gripping at her neck to stop her from pulling away. 

She tasted of ash and green and warmth. She tasted safe; Agatha wanted to drink up the feeling that was usually so foreign to her. 

When she felt something hot and wet against Rio’s cheek, she simply wiped the tear away and kissed her harder. 

“Thank you for telling me,” she said after pulling away. “I hope that one was better than my last.” 

"I was kind of distracted..." Rio laughed, wiping her eyes. "Might need you to do it again so I can confirm." 

Agatha narrowed her eyes.

Rio eventually guided Agatha back to her bedroom; Agatha fell to sleep with soft breaths against her shoulder from Rio pressed against her back. It was peaceful in a way she had only ever known when Nicky was beside her. 

Nothing happened between the two of them. 

She didn’t expect anything to happen between them, besides their shared kisses. 

Not after her behaviour last time.  

But she didn’t leave, no matter how overwhelming the urge. That had to count for something. 

However, waking up to her stomach curdling and skin feeling clammy was definitely ruining the sentiment.

She lay in a bed that was not her own, Rio turned away from her with her breaths still even, and stared at the ceiling as she willed the nausea to pass. 

It did not pass. 

The moment she felt her stomach lurch, she was out from under the covers and running to the bathroom as quickly as possible. She didn’t have time to make it to the room down the hall; her stomach was intent on betraying her secret and subsequently she had to resort to using the en-suite. 

She couldn’t control the sound of her retching, nor the tears that met her cheeks from the exertion. 

“Merry fucking Christmas to me,” she muttered under her breath as she washed her face in the basin, staring at her paling skin in the mirror. She looked rough, yes. Perfectly so that she could pass it off as an awful hangover. 

“Agatha, are you-” the door to the bathroom opened, a worried Rio, still half asleep, stood in the doorway. “Oh, shit.”

“I’m fine,” she said through a deep exhale. 

Rio took in the scene before her; Agatha was surprised to see no disgust and no annoyance on her face. There was only concern. 

“You strike me as the kind of person to not get hangovers.”

Agatha laughed through a deep breath, her hand still gripping the basin of the sink. “There’s a lot you don’t know about me, Vidal.” 

Rio didn’t answer. Instead, she moved into the room and took a small washcloth from a drawer. She dabbed the damp cloth to Agatha’s forehead and in turn, Agatha’s shoulders eased in tandem with her grip on the sink. 

“You need to eat,” Rio murmured. 

Agatha’s stomach lurched at the thought.

She allowed herself to be walked out of the bathroom, through the bedroom and into the living area.

The light hurt her eyes, so much so that even she could be convinced she was suffering from a hangover and not at the hands of a person the size of a goddamn grape. 

Rio guided her to sit down on one of the stools of her breakfast bar.

Agatha watched the way her eyes never left her own, concern etched into her features. Rio was gentle when she moved a loose lock of hair behind Agatha’s ear, and even gentler when she placed a kiss on Agatha's cheek. 

It only worsened her guilt. 

“I really am enjoying you in my clothes, you know,” she said as she stepped back. 

Agatha rolled her eyes, but it was hard to hide her smile nonetheless. 

“I just vomited everywhere and that’s what you focus on?” 

“What, you look good!” 

Agatha glared at her. 

“Vomit and all,” Rio shrugged. 

“Well, get used to it. I haven’t got anything else to wear today.” 

“Do you want to stop at your place on the way?” 

“No,” Agatha deadpanned. “Absoloutely not. Unless you want me locking the door behind you and hiding in my bed for the rest of the day.” 

“That could be fun,” Rio’s lips curved into a smile. “Minus the kicking me out part.” 

She would have argued back if it weren’t for the wave of nausea rolling through her stomach. Rio noticed the slight gag she held back, and poured her a glass of water. 

“Ok, drink that. What can you eat? Bagel? Eggs?”

Agatha shook her head, grimacing at the thought. “Bread. Plain bread.” 

“Jesus, how much did you drink? You didn’t even seem that-” 

“Rio,” Agatha warned before taking a sip of her water.

Truth was, she felt like utter shit. Dizzy, cold, and like the only place she should be visiting that day was the bathroom.

The reality of the situation - of the secret she was keeping - hit her hard.

Because spending the night with Rio had been good. Really good. It felt easy, and natural. There was no pressure, and Agatha hadn’t felt for one moment that it wasn’t right. 

Until she remembered that it could never work between them. What she was building right now was a false promise. 

Rio barely trusted her enough now as it was.

To find out that Agatha had been keeping such a secret from her - a secret that she was allowing Rio no control over - would change everything between them before they had gotten a chance to have anything; to be anything. 

Which is why she needed to book an appointment at the clinic as soon as possible.

 

 

It had been a task in itself to make it to Lilia’s house without throwing up her guts in their cab. It had been an even harder task to mentally prepare herself for the day ahead and the fact that Rio Vidal was still sitting right beside her. 

Rio would say differently, but Agatha wanted to leave the moment they had arrived. She felt shit and Lilia kept looking at her with a questioning eye that made her wish the ground would swallow her up. When Rio had made a joke about Agatha being hungover, Lilia had simply frowned at her and proceeded to focus on their guest. 

Lilia took Rio into the kitchen swiftly, under the guise that she wanted to show Rio their drink selection, and what she had been preparing for their festive dinner.

Agatha knew it was merely a plan to interrogate Rio further but she let it happen. It allowed for the necessary time she needed to check the house for anything dead-child related in the hope of avoiding any awkward conversations between them later. 

Thankfully, it seemed Lilia had taken heed of her demands from the night before; the pictures and old artwork scattered on walls and shelves had been removed whilst his old raincoat and shoes that always stayed where he had last left them by the coat rack, had ended up in the back of the downstairs closet. 

For all the ways Lilia managed to get under Agatha’s skin, she appreciated the sentiment of what the woman had done. She knew it was not easy for Lilia to remove any of those things, just like it was not easy for Agatha to see them again. 

One thing Agatha hadn’t prepared herself for was how quickly it took for Rio to become besotted by Lilia’s cat. He wasn’t good with strangers and would often hide under a bed upstairs or hiss at anyone that dared approach him. 

Not with Rio. 

With Rio, he walked right up to her when she held out her hands. He sniffed it and nuzzled his face against her knuckles.

From that moment on, they were inseparable and Agatha had to hide the splintering of her heart as she watched them together. 

In some weird, highly over-sensitive way - by her standards anyway - Agatha felt like it was Nicky’s way of giving his approval to her. 

“Ah. Rabbit doesn’t just take to anyone, you know,” Lilia mentioned as she walked into the living room to find Rio with said cat cradled in her arms. 

When Agatha heard the name that had slipped from Lilia’s mouth, she couldn’t help but glare at her, because how were they going to explain that one without appearing utterly insane? 

“I’m sorry. You named your cat, Rabbit?” 

And there it was. 

“You gave a spider a human name, I don’t think you have a leg to stand on here,” Agatha interrupted quickly, forcing a nonchalance that did not come naturally to her amidst the sheer panic of slipping up. 

“A spider?” Lilia took the opportunity to divert the conversation, sitting down beside them in her armchair.  “I knew you were interesting. Tell us more.” 

“Victoria is a tarantula, actually. I do keep telling Agatha,” Rio offered her a side-eyed glance before turning back to Lilia. “She’s nineteen years old, and yeah. We’ve been through a lot together.” 

Agatha couldn’t help but be reminded of their shared conversation the night before.

Ever since she had found out why Rio had chosen to keep such a creature, she felt regret over all the times she had chastised her for having it. 

“Ah,” Lilia smiled considerately. “The weavers of life and death; fortune too. She’ll help you create your own reality.” 

Rio stifled a laugh. “She better hurry up then, we’re both running out of time.” 

It was Agatha’s turn to laugh.

If only she had too little time as well. 

The rest of the day passed without any further hiccups; dinner was a pleasant enough affair despite Agatha having to breathe through waves of sickness every two minutes. Rio got on too well with Lilia, which would usually have pissed Agatha off but she was silently thankful at the attention being diverted from her. When Lilia got up to clear their plates, Rio snuck a kiss to Agatha’s cheek. 

It was nice. 

Normal, in fact. 

Normal, until Lilia came back into the room. 

Agatha and Rio had moved back into the living area. They sat on the couch together, Rio’s hand resting upon Agatha’s knee as she nursed a glass of Irish cream whilst Agatha stuck to water.

Agatha had just started to lean her head against Rio’s shoulder when Lilia’s head popped around the frame of the door, eyes set on her. 

“Can you help me with the dishes?” 

Rio went to move from the couch, but Lilia held her hand up. 

“No, no dear. You’ve done enough, enjoy your drink for a while,” she said with a kind face that disappeared the moment her gaze returned to Agatha. “You,” she pointed, “come on.” 

Agatha sighed. A heavy feeling formed in her stomach, a mixture of the nausea now accompanied by dread.

She knew Lilia too well; the woman was displeased about something and Agatha was about to find out whether she liked it or not. 

There was silence between them for several minutes as Lilia continued to wash the crockery and Agatha stood beside her, drying each plate and knife with her breath held. 

“When were you going to tell me?” 

She didn’t look up at Agatha when she spoke, nor did she stop what she was doing when Agatha stilled entirely. 

“Excuse me?” 

“I know what you are hiding, like I know that you’re not hungover because I also know that you did not drink a drop of alcohol last night.” 

The plate that dropped from Agatha’s grasp and smashed into the sink was the only noise to break through their silence. 

Lilia didn’t even flinch. She turned to Agatha, eyes alight with a rage that would remain unspoken. For now. 

“When,” she spoke through gritted teeth, “were you going to tell me?” 

“No,” Agatha stepped away, throwing the dish cloth to the counter and locking her hands within each other to stop them shaking. “We are not doing this now. Not here.” 

Lilia looked her up and down, her mouth opening slightly. 

Agatha's chest heaved erratically as her fists clenched together. "I'm going to fucking kill Jen-"

“So, it is true,” she whispered under her breath. 

Lilia didn’t need confirmation; Agatha’s face said it all. 

“Jen told you, didn't she?” 

Lilia shook her head; Agatha growled. 

Lilia slipped her hands free from her washing up gloves, her face softening slightly as she moved closer to Agatha. She pulled out a chair from the small dining table and beckoned Agatha to sit down, to which she accepted without another word. 

“My cards. Darling, you should know this by now. How far along are you?”

“We are not talking about this. Not now, not here. Especially with Rio-”

“Agatha,” Lilia interrupted. “How far?” 

The shaking palm of her hand came to rest on her forehead. She stared at the floor, trying to catch her breath. 

“Just over two months.” 

Lilia’s eyes widened, before she smiled to herself. “That makes a lot of sense.” 

Agatha spat out a laugh. “What?” 

“Again, my cards. Catch up.” 

The last thing Agatha was going to do was ask Lilia what cards she had pulled.

“It's not my place to enquire, but I thought you had stopped with the men… a long time ago.” 

“Oh my god, Lilia. Please shut up,” she hissed through gritted teeth. 

Agatha kept glancing to the hallway to check it was clear. 

“Forgive me for my confusion, but I believe you’ve shown up here with a very pretty woman who I also believe you spent last night with… It’s only natural to ask.” 

“I-” Agatha exhaled as she finally looked at Lilia. “I have. I did. The men, I mean. That’s the problem.” 

“... What are you trying to say?” 

“That this is physically impossible; there has been no man involved.”

Lilia remained still, processing what she had just been told.

Agatha’s fingernails dug into the skin of her forearm.

She didn’t want to hear whatever was about to come out of Lilia’s mouth because the woman knew too much about anything and everything. 

The worst part? 

She was always right. 

“But there was a woman involved?” 

Agatha stared at her. It wasn’t often that she was lost for words, but right now was one of them.

All she could do was nod as blood began to pool around the fingernail she had just unknowingly ripped the skin from. 

“And it was…” Lilia indicated her head to the hallway, where Rio sat several rooms away. 

Her cheeks began to redden when she offered Lilia a second nod. 

“Has she spoken to you about her craft?” 

“What?” 

Lilia looked at her like it was the most obvious question in the world. 

“What kind of witch is she, Agatha?” 

Agatha looked at her, mouth parted slightly. 

She laughed through her disbelief. 

Not a single "are you ok?"

Not a hint of compassion. 

Nothing. 

“Forgive me for not knowing but I’ve had more pressing issues to be concerned with,” Agatha remarked with venom. “I don't appreciate you springing this upon me. Especially not on today of all days. This conversation can be had another time, not that it is needed. But you wouldn't know that because you've not bothered to ask, have you? I’ve already made my decision and the last thing I need is for this conversation to be intruded on. It’s all just unnecessary-” 

Lilia’s lips pursed together. “Your decision?” 

“Doing it once was enough.” 

“Agatha, you can’t be serious-” 

“I am and that is final,” she snapped after standing from her seat. “I think it’s best we leave. Merry Christmas.” 

Lilia stood, dumbfounded, as Agatha left the room.

 

 

Of course, Agatha didn’t tell Rio the truth about why they were leaving. She made up some excuse about Lilia liking to have alone time but always being too polite to say so, and the two of them were swiftly in a cab and leaving the suburbs. 

Agatha also omitted the truth when Rio enquired about their questionable choice in tree decorations. 

“It was like a kid painted them-” 

“Lilia used to work with kids. The class made them for her one year and she insists on having them hung every Christmas without fail.” 

It hurt to lie about that. 

It hurt to hear Rio say ‘a kid’ like he was no one and not her entire world. 

It hurt the most when Rio tried to ask Agatha to come back to her apartment again.

It hurt because Agatha wanted that nearly more than anything but it was Nicky who came above anything and everything and that meant not choosing Rio. Again. 

“There’s something I have to do,” Agatha told her in the back of their taxi. “I want to, believe me, but I can’t tonight.” 

“You’ll still come to New Years though, right?” 

Right. 

Rio was hosting a New Year’s Eve get-together - labelled that simply for the fact that Rio claimed she didn’t have enough friends to call it a party. That had made Agatha feel even worse about trying to get out of it, so she had agreed through gritted teeth. 

Agatha nodded with a small smile. "I'll be there. And if, out of interest, you’re not doing anything on Wednesday then I will be working from home. If you want to get coffee, or lunch, or whatever people do these days, that doesn't end up with us in bed together then I will be around. Not that I don’t want to end up in bed with you, but considering-”  

“Yes, I would love that,” Rio interrupted as she stared at Agatha with those doe-eyes that she had not seen since the night before. 

They made her feel disgustingly giddy inside. 

That, or she needed to throw-up again. 

They reached Agatha’s apartment first, and Rio tried not to look too impressed by the grandeur of the building or the fact that it had an actual doorman standing outside. 

“Of course you live somewhere like this,” Rio shook her head in disbelief. 

Agatha offered a small laugh as she collected her bag and opened her car door, before turning back and meeting Rio’s eyes that were already plastered on her.

“Goodnight, Rio. I’ll see you on Wednesday.” 

“Can I kiss you again?” 

She nearly felt her cheeks begin to redden.

“You can.” 

So Rio did. 

“Goodnight, Agatha. See you Wednesday.”

The moment she got through her apartment door, she lobbed her heels from her feet in two different directions, threw her bag to the floor and collapsed onto one of her couches with a deep exhale. 

She couldn’t quite process everything that had happened in the last twenty-four hours. 

A borderline love confession; a confrontation from divination itself; throwing her guts up from something she didn’t even want.

And she hadn’t even undergone the trauma of visiting her son yet. 

When the tears wet her cheeks - hot and quick - she slapped her palm against her thigh and clenched her fists in frustration before screaming into the cushion she slammed into her face. 

Her mind was in overdrive and she had no means of escape. 

The only way she knew to escape, without alcohol or cigarettes or smashing her face into a mirror, was what would inevitably continue to keep her trapped in this cycle forever: Nicky. 

But Agatha Harkness was never known for helping herself. 

So she took herself back to the darkness of her closet.

 

 

25th December, 2018

She found herself back in a room where the sheets smelled of old memories and the walls offered a security that was now alien to her.

Her old bedroom in Lilia’s home. It had been converted to a guest bedroom when her and Nicky had moved out just before his first birthday, but she had continued to stay in it each year afterwards. 

She remembered late-night journalling and last-minute attempts to meet deadlines sat at the desk that had now been turned to a vanity unity.

She remembered the several, grainy ultrasound pictures that hung above it, now replaced by artwork that could never carry the same meaning.

She could feel those infant cries at three in the morning from the crib next to where she slept, now gone and only the rug remaining soft under her feet. 

Time was cruel and no one knew that better than Agatha Harkness. 

The alarm clock read 5.32am, but she didn’t need to know the time to know what would happen next. She remembered every second of that day already. 

So she got into the bed and waited. 

Sure enough, barely five minutes later, the gentle patter of small feet across the hallway met her ears. She had missed that sound. 

She had missed even more the clumsy knock at her door and the way it opened slowly before a mischievous smile showed itself. 

“Mama,” Nicky whispered into the darkness. “Wake up?” 

Agatha inhaled at the sound of her boy’s voice, hands gripping at the sheets before she sat up to see him properly.

His hair was mussed from sleep, his pyjamas printed with monkeys and jungle leaves. She remembered he had picked them out at the store himself despite their trip being to purchase some festive ones for that year; she hadn’t had the heart to say no to him. 

“I’m awake, baby,” she said. “Come here.” 

That was all he needed to hear before launching himself onto the bed and reaching for her. She held her arms out and he fell into them; his head found her chest as she breathed in the sweet scent of a time that no longer existed. 

Finally, after everything she had endured the last twenty-four hours, her shoulders eased and her breath evened. There was something about a mother holding their child; the instant calm that washed over you. Nothing could hurt you with your baby in your arms. 

Which left her with the thought: how? 

How can I do this again? 

How can I love someone that is not him? 

It wouldn’t be possible. 

She had barely trusted herself enough to love Nicky; if it weren’t for Lilia, she doubted she would have ever gone through with it in the first place. 

Now she endured a life of mourning. Now she spent the rest of her days wondering why she couldn’t have saved him. 

She couldn’t go through that again. 

“Did Santa come? I heard noises in the night,” Nicky said, ”It was reindeer!”

Nicky spoke too quickly, wriggling in his mother's arms; Agatha held him so tightly he had no chance of escape as she laughed at his conviction. 

“Mama, downstairs now?”

She groaned, nuzzling her face into his neck. “Mama needs more cuddles first.” 

She rocked him, she tickled him. He giggled. She hid the tears on her cheeks. 

Nicky managed until 5.52 am before Agatha was holding him at her hip as they ventured to the living room downstairs. 

“Mama, look!” 

Nicky’s face when he saw the presents under the tree that morning had been etched into her mind ever since. Her boy smiled so brightly, bouncing up and down on his little feet with the excitement of a toddler who had only just started to understand what Christmas was. 

“I see it, baby. All for you.” 

“For me?” 

Agatha nodded, fighting through the tears welling in her eyes. “You want to have a look?” 

Nicky nodded eagerly, his small fingers reaching for the sleeve of her shirt and dragging her to the tree. She sat down beside him, cross-legged and watching him intently as he held up each present, shaking them and holding them up to his ear. His smile was so, so bright. 

“Can I open them?” 

“Let’s wait for Grandma Lils, hm?”

When she saw him frown but agreed with her nonetheless, she wondered how she had ever got so lucky to have a child so kind, so thoughtful.

It was a far cry from the person she was. 

She wished she could have seen him grow up. He could have been everything she couldn’t. 

“Ok, seeing as somebody,” she eyed Nicky with a theatricism that made him giggle, “woke me up at five-thirty in the morning, I think Mama deserves a coffee. What do you think?” 

“Noooo,” Nicky laughed and squirmed as she gathered him into her lap and peppered kisses over his face. 

“No?” 

She feigned her shock. 

“No!” he squeaked through his laughter. 

“What if I were to say that very well behaved little boys on Christmas morning are allowed one hot chocolate instead of milk?” 

The way Nicky’s face lit up warmed her deeply. 

It was the mundane things that she realised she had taken for granted when he was alive. 

Like carrying him on her hip and feeling his arms around her neck; laughing when cocoa powder spilled everywhere instead of getting frustrated; watching him stir his drink with an intense precision that caused her to wonder how that would have evolved as he had gotten older. 

Lilia walked into the kitchen as Agatha was pouring her coffee. Nicky, who had sat down at the small table with both hands around his mug like it was an important trophy, squealed Lilia’s name and bounced up and down in his chair. 

“Good morning to you, little star,” Lilia smiled. “You didn’t want to let your Mama sleep?” 

“It’s Christmas, Grandma Lils! Santa left presents! Mama said we can open them when you’re here. Mama, can we open them now? Lils, where’s Rabbit?” 

“Nicky, slow down,” Agatha said softly as she leant against the counter with her drink in hand, smiling at his mile-a-minute words. 

Lilia moved to Nicky and placed a kiss on his hair, and in true Nicky fashion, he tried to get away from it with little success. 

“I think he’s hiding from an overly-active three year old.”

“I’m nearly four,” Nicky said, puffing out his chest and straightening his jaw at Lilia’s comment. 

Agatha withheld her laugh despite the squeeze within her heart. 

He never got to turn four. 

“That can’t be right. You are not allowed to grow up anymore,” Lilia pinched his cheeks, invoking another host of giggles from the small child. She moved to the stove and filled the kettle up. “Once I’ve made my tea, we can find Rabbit and open your presents. Sound good?” 

“Yes,” Nicky squealed. “Yes, presents, yes!” 

Rabbit came to join them as soon as they had settled back into the living room. The cat had beelined straight to Nicky, rubbing his head against the boy’s crossed knees and choosing to sit beside him as he opened his presents. 

The floor became filled with discarded gift-wrap, boxes of lego, books and puzzles. 

Agatha had spent the entire time watching her son whilst holding back tears. Lilia had glanced in her direction several times, holding her under her gaze, assessing her. 

It was almost as if Lilia knew she belonged from another time. 

It always felt like Lilia knew, whenever she was present in a memory, though neither had discussed it with the other. 

When Nicky opened the stuffed animal in the shape of a rabbit, she couldn’t hold them back any longer.

She excused herself to the bathroom and broke. 

Though he opened it every time she returned, she could never shake the smell of disinfectant or the sound of beeping monitors when she saw the toy. She could not forget how it felt to watch the only reason you had to live, quickly fade away.

After the excitement of the morning, Nicky had climbed himself up onto the couch and into his mother’s lap whilst Lilia left to prepare breakfast.

His head burrowed into her side and she immediately wrapped her arms around him, rocking him slowly as his energy began to wane. 

“Mama, blanket?” 

“I’ll go get it. Stay here, honey.” 

When she came back downstairs, Nicky now had his toy rabbit in his arms.

It was moments like these that made it so necessary for her to return; to be able to see her boy again.

She needed to remember that there had been happy moments before the bad; that his rabbit had once been a symbol of his joy and not his own demise. 

“Scoot over, mister,” Agatha said as she reclaimed her place on the couch and pulled her son back into her arms before tucking his patchwork quilt around them both. 

After they were comfortable, Nicky held up the rabbit in Agatha’s face. “This is Winnie.” 

“Winnie the rabbit? And where did you get that name from?” 

“Winnie the Pooh!” 

Agatha laughed. “Darling, we need to practice your animals more. Winnie is a bear, not a rabbit.” 

“But this rabbit is Winnie!”

“Great, so now we have a cat named after a rabbit and a rabbit named after a bear. What’s next? A bear named after a mouse?” 

Nicky giggled, pressing his shaking head closer into her shoulder. “That would be silly.” 

“I’m glad you agree,” she said through her breathless chuckles as she wiped his hair from his forehead and placed a kiss there, “Ok. Winnie it is.” 

As her child dozed off in her arms, she looked around the room she had only been in that same day, in the present.

It had changed and had not changed in the six years that separated them.

The furniture was the same, Nicky’s baubles still decorated the tree in the corner whilst the sound of Lilia pottering in the kitchen played out in the background. Even the damn cat was still there. 

But not Nicky. 

It was the first Christmas he would remember, and the last he’d ever get to experience. 

In that moment she knew that she needed only these memories to look back on; she did not need, or want, to make new ones.

Not with a child that wasn’t him.

 

 

It was hard to see her son at anytime. 

It was hardest to see him at Christmas. 

It was an indescribable pain to go back to one of the last times their lives as a family had been normal. A few months later and he would be gone from her arms for forever.

The worst part was knowing that she had been none the wiser. Perhaps, if she had known, she wouldn't be returning to a time that no longer existed. 

That day, she was thankful for her gift, no matter how empty it left her feeling afterwards because it only cemented her decision. 

She didn't want a baby; she wanted her baby. 

She couldn't bring another one into the world.

She couldn't be a mother to them like she had been a mother to Nicky.

She couldn't risk becoming another Evanora Harkness. 

Not after everything that had happened. 

If only she had known that in four days time, she would start to bleed.

Chapter 7

Chapter Summary

Agatha starts bleeding. Rio hosts NYE.

TW around the topic of miscarriages!

Chapter Notes

i'm several beers deep whilst editing and have been close to tears writing this entire chapter so good luck

(post editing and wow there is a huge portion of this chapter that is written appallingly so pretend it doesn't sound so robotic and tell instead show vibes and i'll come back to edit another day as im not impressed with it at all)

See the end of the chapter for more notes

After Christmas, work had been hellish. 

It always was at that time of the year. Whilst other corporate leeches got to indulge in a week of limbo between the New Year, Agatha was stuck at her desk until nine in the evening on Monday, and quarter to ten on Tuesday. 

She was mid-investigation into an embezzlement case with no one ballsy enough to go on the record with her, and her team had proven to be more incompetent than ever after one reporter swore at their interviewee and her copy editor allowed the wrong title to be published, resulting in an incredible social media storm that she had to pick up the pieces of. 

Which was why Wednesday was supposed to be a day for her. 

She was working from home which meant a day of no heels, minimal stress and zero interns having the audacity to talk to her. 

Plus, the thought of seeing Rio had actually excited her once. 

She needed to laugh; to breathe. 

Rio could provide that better than anyone else in her life currently. 

Rio had been texting her; that had helped get through the first half of the week. 

It had been casual between them but she enjoyed having someone to talk to at the end of the day. She had even found herself staring at her screen, unlocking her phone several times just to see if she had a notification from her yet. 

When she caught herself doing it, she would lock her phone in the drawer. 

They had planned to meet at a cafe closer to Rio in Brooklyn. Agatha had chosen to make the journey, despite Rio’s protests about her having to work. She hadn’t been too nauseous the day before and had said a silent prayer that today would offer her the same grace. 

The first thing she should have noticed was her inability to wake up that morning despite it being nearly nine-thirty. The second thing she should have noticed was the dull ache in her back when she finally arose from bed at ten. 

That was when the cramping started. 

It was when she tried to get out of bed after ten minutes of the pain radiating within her, that she realised there was an uncomfortable warmth between her legs. 

That was when her heart started trying to escape from her ears. 

When she saw the stain on her sheets, her stomach lurched. 

She had never been bad with blood but the sight of the sickly red colour that day shook her in a way she had not been prepared for. 

She hadn’t anticipated the pain of it all. Not the physical pain, but a deep, dull pain that seeped into her nerves and thudded against her chest as she realised what was happening. 

She was losing her baby. 

Again. 

“No,” she whispered. “No, no, no.” 

Her head was foggy with panic. Her knees buckled and she fell to the floor against the side of her bed. Her fingers shook as she reached for her phone on the bedside table. 

It was in those moments of hesitation over Jen’s contact name, or Lilia’s, or straight-up dialling 911, that she remembered each moment she had willed the life away that was inside her before it had even begun. 

Now it would never get the chance to. 

That was when she realised. 

“This can’t be happening.”

Her hand clutched against her abdomen, as if she could make them stay.

“I want more time,” she cried out to no one in particular, trying to see through her blurring vision. “Please, don't go. Please.” 

Who was she kidding? 

She couldn’t save her child the first time, and she wouldn’t be able to for a second. 

She dialled Jen’s number as she wiped away the hot, wet tears against her cheeks. 

“Jen?” 

“Nice of you to pick up the phone for once,” came Jen’s voice through the line. 

Agatha controlled her exhale. “I’m bleeding, Jen.” 

Jen was quiet for several seconds. It only made the panic in Agatha’s throat worsen. 

“Oh.”

“Yes, oh.” 

“Are you in pain?” 

She wanted to laugh. How did she tell her that the physical pain was nothing in comparison to the earth-shattering realisation that she was losing a baby she didn’t want to lose, despite having cursed its entire existence? 

“I have cramps,” she answered instead. 

“You need to go to the ER immediately, Agatha. I’ll meet you there.” 

“No,” she said quickly. “No hospitals.” 

“Agatha, this is serious.” 

She was split in two. She hadn’t stepped foot inside a hospital in over five years, and she wasn’t about to make her grand return by having one claim a second child from her. 

“I can’t go back there,” she said in a voice so small she wasn’t even sure it belonged to her. “Don’t make me go back there.” 

“Ok,” Jen said through a deep breath. “Ok, I’m coming to get you. We’ll do an ultrasound here.” 

“You’re not too busy?” 

“Of course I’m busy, but no one else is bleeding out on my table right now.” 

“I’m not bleeding out, Jen,” Agatha’s words were decisive. “This can just happen, right? It can happen and mean nothing… right?” 

Jen was silent. 

“I don’t know what you want me to say,” she eventually replied. 

Agatha’s free hand clenched into a fist, exhaling shakily. 

“I want you to tell me I’m not losing another child, damn it!” 

Jen’s lack of reply was an answer in itself. 

The quiet stretched between them. The burning in her throat from her raised voice gave way to a broken, strangled sound as tears met her cheeks again. 

“Jen, I don’t want to lose them,” she whispered into the phone. “I know what I said, but I-” she paused, catching her breath. “...I-”

“I know. I’m coming now, just sit tight ok?” 

She nodded, not that Jen could see her. The phone fell limply from her grasp onto the counter as she let her knees give way, her body falling to the floor against the wall. 

All she could do was wait. 

Wait and watch her child slip away for the second time.

 

 

The journey to Jen’s clinic had been a blur to say the least. 

Jen had asked Agatha a myriad of questions; all of which she had been borderline mute in answering. 

Was she still nauseous? She’d be concerned if she wasn’t considering the morning she had endured. 

Had she spoken to Rio? She had not exactly had the chance. 

Was she ok? Absolutely not. 

It was weird to be back in the place it had all started; to be in the place where she had found out the news she was sure she didn’t want to be real only to be here now and realise she had never been more wrong. 

She felt so much worse that time, she couldn’t even offer a snide remark to the receptionist.

She kept her head down as Jen guided her to her office. Each step was a silent beg to whatever God may exist above to please not let her lose this child. 

When she lay against the cold sheets of the examination table, time itself suspended into liminal space she had only found herself in once before. She remembered those final days, the nurses' sorrowful attempts at preparing her for the worst, watching the minutes move forward against her little boy’s favour and having no power herself to stop it. 

Her heart thudded in her ears, her skin a pale gray that she was not used to. 

“Ok,” Jen opened the privacy curtain and wheeled in the ultrasound machine. “You sure you’re ok with this?” 

“Let my best friend shove her hand up my vagina? I’m enamoured by it,” she remarked with a bitterness Jen ignored. “Just do what you need to do.” 

She had already accepted the worst. 

She just needed Jen to tell her she was not dying so she could go home, cry in peace and forget that any of this ever happened. 

The gel was cold, the probe uncomfortable. 

Jen was clinical and precise, like Agatha was just another patient. 

“Are you comfortable?” Jen asked. 

Agatha couldn’t look at her as she nodded. Her arms were above her head, covering her face as she willed this entire experience to just be over already. 

She was far from comfortable. 

Then again, it was what she deserved. 

“Agatha,” Jen’s voice met her ears from where she hid. “Agatha, look at me.” 

She took her arms away from her face slowly, hands still covering her eyes slightly. 

“Do you want to see your baby?” 

Those were the last words she expected to come out of Jen's mouth. She was sure they were a cruel joke, or that Jen actually wanted her to look at her dead child. 

But Jen was smiling, soft and sincere. Her eyes were bright. She could just make out the tear threatening to escape the corner of one of them. 

“What?” Agatha whispered. 

“There’s a heartbeat.” 

“You’re lying.” 

Jen shook her head. 

She pointed towards the screen after turning it around for Agatha to see. 

There was the smallest shape, not quite a blob, not quite a formed person, but they were real and they were alive. She could make out their head, the way their spine arched as they curled into themselves. She could see the tiniest hand, and tiniest leg.  

She could see the small thump of their heart.

Agatha could see her baby. 

Alive. 

That was the moment that changed everything for her. She needed this baby; she needed them alive. She wanted to be their mother. She wanted to give them everything she couldn’t give to Nicky. She wanted to let Nicky’s memory live on in his brother or sister. 

She wanted another chance. 

“They’re ok?” 

She couldn’t take her eyes from the screen, worried that if she did, they would disappear altogether. 

Jen nodded. “They are. Everything is looking good… sometimes our bodies tell us when we need to slow down, and I think yours was doing that today.” 

Agatha wiped at her eye, a soft laugh escaping her mouth. “Great, they have opinions already,” she muttered under her breath. 

“Seeing as you’re here. Finally,” Jen flashed a condescending smile, “I can confirm you’re measuring at around ten weeks, which…” 

Agatha grimaced. “Yep. That adds up.” 

“So, it’s Rio’s?” 

“Well I'm sure I wasn’t drugged and kidnapped, and there was no one else so-” 

“I’ll take that as a yes then,” Jen said. “You mentioned you’d made a decision?” 

“Let’s not talk about that,” Agatha murmured, looking away. 

She turned back to the screen, watching her baby inside her, barely bigger than a strawberry that had somehow managed to rewire her entire being. “My decision is them.”
